========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there frightened or worrying over one particular thing. It’s just that if I sit down, almost right away I feel pulled to get up and do something—straighten a drawer, fix some little thing, rearrange something on the table, or think, “Oh, I should call about that volunteer task.”

It’s not even always important things. Sometimes I’ll catch myself puttering around the house and think, “Why am I doing this right now?” But being still just feels uncomfortable, like I’m wasting time or like my body hasn’t quite settled. Even at my age, when everyone tells me to rest more, I have a hard time just staying put.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hmm… I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has its little ups and downs like it does at my age, but I can’t say I’ve had some obvious unintentional weight loss that made me worry.

As for feeling unusually hot or sweaty, not really. I mean, I’ll get warm if I’m bustling around too much, but that seems more because I’m up doing things. I haven’t noticed myself just sitting there overheating or sweating for no reason.

It’s like if I sit down, I’m already thinking of the next thing I should get up and do.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can't sit still, is it because you're worrying about something or feeling anxious, or is it more just a physical urge or need to keep moving even when your mind isn't especially worried?\n\nPatient: It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there frightened or worrying over one particular thing. It’s just that if I sit down, almost right away I feel pulled to get up and do something—straighten a drawer, fix some little thing, rearrange something on the table, or think, “Oh, I should call about that volunteer task.”\n\nIt’s not even always important things. Sometimes I’ll catch myself puttering around the house and think, “Why am I doing this right now?” But being still just feels uncomfortable, like I’m wasting time or like my body hasn’t quite settled. Even at my age, when everyone tells me to rest more, I have a hard time just staying put.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other new prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to anything like that, no. I mean, I do have the usual sorts of medicines people my age end up with, but I don’t remember some new pill being started right when all this restlessness began, and I don’t recall anything like an anti-nausea medicine or one of those stronger psychiatric medicines being added.\n\nI’d probably have to look at my list to be absolutely certain, because I don’t want to tell you wrong, but nothing stands out to me as, “Oh yes, that’s when it started.” It feels more like this has just been… me getting more and more unable to sit still, not something I clearly tied to a new prescription.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually hot or sweaty, a racing heartbeat, or hand tremors over this past year?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has its little ups and downs like it does at my age, but I can’t say I’ve had some obvious unintentional weight loss that made me worry.\n\nAs for feeling unusually hot or sweaty, not really. I mean, I’ll get warm if I’m bustling around too much, but that seems more because I’m up doing things. I haven’t noticed myself just sitting there overheating or sweating for no reason.\n\nA racing heartbeat… I don’t think so. I haven’t had that feeling like my heart is pounding away out of nowhere. And I haven’t noticed my hands trembling, either—not in a way that stands out to me. If you mean a little shakiness now and then, I suppose anyone can have that, but nothing I’d call a tremor or anything new that I’ve been paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed your mood feeling unusually elevated, euphoric, or irritable much of the time, separate from the restlessness itself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it elevated or euphoric, no. I’m certainly not going around feeling unusually wonderful or on top of the world. If anything, I can get a bit short-tempered sometimes when I’m interrupted or when I feel like things aren’t getting done, but I’m not sure that’s a whole mood shift so much as me being impatient in the moment.\n\nOver the past year, I haven’t noticed a stretch where I was just unusually cheerful or high, or angry all the time for no clear reason. I’d say my mood has been fairly ordinary for me—some good days, some cranky moments, but nothing that feels separate and unusual from the restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed that you need much less sleep than usual—like feeling rested after only a few hours—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, my sleep isn’t perfect—at my age I may wake up, or have nights where I’m not sleeping as soundly as I’d like—but I wouldn’t say I’ve been needing much less sleep and then feeling perfectly fine the next day.\n\nIf I only sleep a few hours, I usually feel it. I don’t have that thing where I’m up all night and then somehow bright-eyed and rested like nothing happened. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of change over the past year.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t recognize those names right off—metoclopramide, prochlorperazine. They sound familiar in the way a lot of medicine names do, but I couldn’t tell you that I definitely took them.

It’s like if I sit down, I’m already thinking of the next thing I should get up and do.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can't sit still, is it because you're worrying about something or feeling anxious, or is it more just a physical urge or need to keep moving even when your mind isn't especially worried?\n\nPatient: It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there frightened or worrying over one particular thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more of a whole-body restlessness, I think, not so much that creepy-crawly feeling in my legs you’re describing. I don’t usually think, “Oh, my legs are aching and I need to move them,” exactly.

It’s more like when I’m sitting at the senior center meetings, or sometimes during the church announcements when they go on a bit, I get this ants-in-my-chair feeling. Like I just can’t quite stay settled. I’ll shift around in the seat, or I’ll decide I need to stand up and get coffee, or I’ll straighten the papers in my bag or on the table—just little things to get through sitting there. It’s not really only in my legs; it feels more like I need to do something with myself.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me come in is that I just can’t seem to stop moving, if that makes sense. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always puttering around—fixing something that probably doesn’t need fixing, arranging things, straightening up, saying yes to one more little volunteer thing. And I know I’m 87, so people expect me to sit down more, rest, take it easy, but I have such a hard time just sitting still. It’s like if I sit down, I’m already thinking of the next thing I should get up and do.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can't sit still, is it because you're worrying about something or feeling anxious, or is it more just a physical urge or need to keep moving even when your mind isn't especially worried?\n\nPatient: It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there frightened or worrying over one particular thing. It’s just that if I sit down, almost right away I feel pulled to get up and do something—straighten a drawer, fix some little thing, rearrange something on the table, or think, “Oh, I should call about that volunteer task.”\n\nIt’s not even always important things. Sometimes I’ll catch myself puttering around the house and think, “Why am I doing this right now?” But being still just feels uncomfortable, like I’m wasting time or like my body hasn’t quite settled. Even at my age, when everyone tells me to rest more, I have a hard time just staying put.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other new prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to anything like that, no. I mean, I do have the usual sorts of medicines people my age end up with, but I don’t remember some new pill being started right when all this restlessness began, and I don’t recall anything like an anti-nausea medicine or one of those stronger psychiatric medicines being added.\n\nI’d probably have to look at my list to be absolutely certain, because I don’t want to tell you wrong, but nothing stands out to me as, “Oh yes, that’s when it started.” It feels more like this has just been… me getting more and more unable to sit still, not something I clearly tied to a new prescription.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually hot or sweaty, a racing heartbeat, or hand tremors over this past year?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has its little ups and downs like it does at my age, but I can’t say I’ve had some obvious unintentional weight loss that made me worry.\n\nAs for feeling unusually hot or sweaty, not really. I mean, I’ll get warm if I’m bustling around too much, but that seems more because I’m up doing things. I haven’t noticed myself just sitting there overheating or sweating for no reason.\n\nA racing heartbeat… I don’t think so. I haven’t had that feeling like my heart is pounding away out of nowhere. And I haven’t noticed my hands trembling, either—not in a way that stands out to me. I’m certainly not going around feeling unusually wonderful or on top of the world. If anything, I can get a bit short-tempered sometimes when I’m interrupted or when I feel like things aren’t getting done, but I’m not sure that’s a whole mood shift so much as me being impatient in the moment.\n\nOver the past year, I haven’t noticed a stretch where I was just unusually cheerful or high, or angry all the time for no clear reason. I don’t have that thing where I’m up all night and then somehow bright-eyed and rested like nothing happened. I don’t recognize those names right off—metoclopramide, prochlorperazine. I’ve never thought of myself as having been on anything like that. So I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never if it was given briefly somewhere, but nothing comes to mind that I knowingly took.\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone close to you noticed any stiffness in your muscles, slower movements, or a tremor in your hands especially when they're at rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\n(none — this is your first inference)\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, welcome in—I'm Dr. Smith. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose what finally made me come in is that I just can’t seem to stop moving, if that makes sense. For the past year or so, I feel like I’m always puttering around—fixing something that probably doesn’t need fixing, arranging things, straightening up, saying yes to one more little volunteer thing. And I know I’m 87, so people expect me to sit down more, rest, take it easy, but I have such a hard time just sitting still. It’s like if I sit down, I’m already thinking of the next thing I should get up and do.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can't sit still, is it because you're worrying about something or feeling anxious, or is it more just a physical urge or need to keep moving even when your mind isn't especially worried?\n\nPatient: It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there frightened or worrying over one particular thing. It’s just that if I sit down, almost right away I feel pulled to get up and do something—straighten a drawer, fix some little thing, rearrange something on the table, or think, “Oh, I should call about that volunteer task.”\n\nIt’s not even always important things. Sometimes I’ll catch myself puttering around the house and think, “Why am I doing this right now?” But being still just feels uncomfortable, like I’m wasting time or like my body hasn’t quite settled. Even at my age, when everyone tells me to rest more, I have a hard time just staying put.\n\nDoctor: Have you started or changed any medications recently, such as antipsychotics, anti-nausea drugs, or other new prescriptions, around the time this restlessness began?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to anything like that, no. I mean, I do have the usual sorts of medicines people my age end up with, but I don’t remember some new pill being started right when all this restlessness began, and I don’t recall anything like an anti-nausea medicine or one of those stronger psychiatric medicines being added.\n\nI’d probably have to look at my list to be absolutely certain, because I don’t want to tell you wrong, but nothing stands out to me as, “Oh yes, that’s when it started.” It feels more like this has just been… me getting more and more unable to sit still, not something I clearly tied to a new prescription.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any unintentional weight loss, feeling unusually hot or sweaty, a racing heartbeat, or hand tremors over this past year?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I don’t think so, at least not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has its little ups and downs like it does at my age, but I can’t say I’ve had some obvious unintentional weight loss that made me worry.\n\nAs for feeling unusually hot or sweaty, not really. I mean, I’ll get warm if I’m bustling around too much, but that seems more because I’m up doing things. I haven’t noticed myself just sitting there overheating or sweating for no reason.\n\nA racing heartbeat… I don’t think so. I haven’t had that feeling like my heart is pounding away out of nowhere. And I haven’t noticed my hands trembling, either—not in a way that stands out to me. If you mean a little shakiness now and then, I suppose anyone can have that, but nothing I’d call a tremor or anything new that I’ve been paying attention to.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed your mood feeling unusually elevated, euphoric, or irritable much of the time, separate from the restlessness itself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d call it elevated or euphoric, no. I’m certainly not going around feeling unusually wonderful or on top of the world. If anything, I can get a bit short-tempered sometimes when I’m interrupted or when I feel like things aren’t getting done, but I’m not sure that’s a whole mood shift so much as me being impatient in the moment.\n\nOver the past year, I haven’t noticed a stretch where I was just unusually cheerful or high, or angry all the time for no clear reason. I’d say my mood has been fairly ordinary for me—some good days, some cranky moments, but nothing that feels separate and unusual from the restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Over this past year, have you noticed that you need much less sleep than usual—like feeling rested after only a few hours—without feeling tired the next day?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, my sleep isn’t perfect—at my age I may wake up, or have nights where I’m not sleeping as soundly as I’d like—but I wouldn’t say I’ve been needing much less sleep and then feeling perfectly fine the next day.\n\nIf I only sleep a few hours, I usually feel it. I don’t have that thing where I’m up all night and then somehow bright-eyed and rested like nothing happened. So, no, I haven’t noticed that kind of change over the past year.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever taken an antipsychotic medication or a strong anti-nausea drug like metoclopramide or prochlorperazine at any point in your life, even years ago?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t recognize those names right off—metoclopramide, prochlorperazine. They sound familiar in the way a lot of medicine names do, but I couldn’t tell you that I definitely took them.\n\nAs for antipsychotic medication, I don’t think so, at least not that I’m aware of. I’ve never thought of myself as having been on anything like that. But going back years, you know, sometimes doctors give you something in the hospital or for nausea and you don’t always remember the name. So I wouldn’t want to say absolutely never if it was given briefly somewhere, but nothing comes to mind that I knowingly took.\n\nDoctor: Have you or anyone close to you noticed any stiffness in your muscles, slower movements, or a tremor in your hands especially when they're at rest?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. I mean, at 87 I’m not as quick as I used to be, so sometimes I have to think, “Is this just age?” You know, getting up from a chair or getting going in the morning can feel a little slower, but I wouldn’t describe it as stiffness in my muscles in that kind of obvious way.\n\nAnd I haven’t noticed my hands shaking when they’re just resting in my lap or anything like that. No one close to me has said, “Your hand is trembling,” or “You’re moving strangely,” at least not that I can recall. If you mean a very noticeable tremor or moving unusually slowly, I’d say no, nothing that’s stood out.\n\nDoctor: \n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I may have missed the question just now. Could you say that again for me? Sometimes I think I’m following and then I realize I’m still thinking about the last thing we were talking about.\n\nDoctor: \n\nPatient: I’m sorry, I don’t think I caught the question. Oh, no, I don’t think this is brand-new from just the last year. I mean, maybe I notice it differently now because I’m older and people are sitting around more at meetings and such, but when I really think back, I’ve been this way a long time.

Even in grade school, I can remember teachers getting after me because I’d pop up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to. Or I’d start talking before the other child was finished—talking over people, I suppose you’d call it now. Back then they just said I was “too lively,” or “couldn’t settle down.” I remember that phrase very clearly, being too lively.

So, no, it wasn’t something that suddenly appeared around a year ago. It’s more like it’s been part of my temperament since I was a child, long before I was a teenager.

It’s like if I sit down, I’m already thinking of the next thing I should get up and do.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you can't sit still, is it because you're worrying about something or feeling anxious, or is it more just a physical urge or need to keep moving even when your mind isn't especially worried?\n\nPatient: It’s more like a physical need to keep moving, I think. I mean, at 87 I’m not as quick as I used to be, so sometimes I have to think, “Is this just age?” You know, getting up from a chair or getting going in the morning can feel a little slower, but I wouldn’t describe it as stiffness in my muscles in that kind of obvious way.\n\nAnd I haven’t noticed my hands shaking when they’re just resting in my lap or anything like that. It’s more like it’s been part of my temperament since I was a child, long before I was a teenager."

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S014\",\"name\":\"Constant_Activity\",\"description\":\"Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so I should return the chief complaint symptom code.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S014\",\"name\":\"Constant_Activity\",\"description\":\"Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether the inability to sit still is a physical drive to keep moving, which directly matches the 'on the go' or 'driven by a motor' symptom.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. If you mean a little shakiness now and then, I suppose anyone can have that, but nothing I’d call a tremor or anything new that I’ve been paying attention to.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":[],"denied":["S026"],"reasoning":{"S026":"Patient reports only minor age-related weight fluctuations and explicitly denies obvious unintentional weight loss that caused concern."}}

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think I’d call it elevated or euphoric, no. I’m certainly not going around feeling unusually wonderful or on top of the world. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking. Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI don’t think so, no—not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. I mean, at 87 I’m not as quick as I used to be, so sometimes I have to think, “Is this just age?” You know, getting up from a chair or getting going in the morning can feel a little slower, but I wouldn’t describe it as stiffness in my muscles in that kind of obvious way.\n\nAnd I haven’t noticed my hands shaking when they’re just resting in my lap or anything like that. No one close to me has said, “Your hand is trembling,” or “You’re moving strangely,” at least not that I can recall. If you mean a very noticeable tremor or moving unusually slowly, I’d say no, nothing that’s stood out.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger.
